“喂，琼。”迈克尔说。
“呃，”琼把酒一饮而尽，收了场；“就是这么回事，伙计们。爱信不信；不爱信——也给我受着。
“不过还有一件要告诉你们，”他补上一句，“方才那些你们要是难信，这件更难信：我不信‘酒鬼’那批石头是查利得的。”
“什么？”
这一问，像鞭梢一炸。汉子堆里起了一阵骚动。
“我不信石头是查利得的，”琼宣称。“我说的是‘得’，不是‘拿’。我只知道：他到悉尼时蔫得像条病鱼……把手里的欧珀全卖了。动身那会儿他倒精神。这条我认。‘酒鬼’的石头兴许是他拿了；可有人又黑了他。我原疑是埃米（Emmy）——那个黄毛货，记得吧，同我们一道下去的。她是个货，没跑。我这小媳妇，如今——她可从来……不是那路人！可莫德说不是：埃米见查利兜里没了钱，把他连人带铺盖撵了出去。他手里那一小片石头，卖了个针尖大点的价……我敢起誓。到后一两天，他还来找我借过钱。还为着什么气得暴跳。…

===Original===

If he shook the stones off Rum-Enough, it’s my belief somebody shook them off of him, either in the train or here﻿—or off of Rummy before he got them.﻿ ﻿…”
Several of the men muttered and grunted their protest. But Jun held to his point, and the talk became more general. Jun asked for news of the fields: what had been done, and who was getting the stuff. Somebody said John Armitage had been up and had bought a few nice stones from the Crosses, Pony-Fence, and Bully Bryant.
“Armitage?” Jun said. “He’s always a good man﻿—gives a fair price. He bought my stones, that last lot﻿ ﻿… gave me a hundred pounds for the big knobby. But it fair took my breath away to hear young Sophie Rouminof had gone off with him.”
Michael was standing beside him before the words were well out of his mouth.
“What did you say?” he demanded.
“I’m sorry, Michael,” Jun replied, after a quick, scared glance at the faces of the men about him. “But I took it for granted you all knew, of course. We saw them a good bit together down in Sydney, Maud and me, and she said she saw Sophie on the *Zealanida* the day the boat sailed. Maud was down seeing a friend off, and she saw Sophie and Mr. Armitage on board. She said﻿—”
Michael turned heavily, and swung out of the bar.
Jun looked after him. In the faces of the men he read what a bomb his news had been among them.
“I wouldn’t have said that for a lot,” he said, “if I’d ’ve thought Michael didn’t know. But, Lord, I thought he knew﻿ ﻿… I thought you all knew.”
In the days which followed, as he wandered over the plains in the late afternoon and evening, Michael tried to come to some understanding with himself of what had happened. At first he had been too overcast by the sense of loss to realise more than that Sophie had gone away. But now, beyond her going, he could see the failure of his own effort to control circumstances. He had failed; Sophie had gone; she had left the Ridge.
“God,” he groaned; “with the best intentions in the world, what an awful mess we make of things!”
Michael wondered whether it would have been worse for Sophie if she had gone away with Paul when her mother died. At least, Sophie was older now and better able to take care of herself.
He blamed himself because she had gone away as she had, all the same; the failure of the Ridge to hold her as well as his own failure beat him to the earth.

要是他从‘酒鬼’手里晃走了石头，依我看，就有人又从他手里晃走了——不是在火车上，就是在这儿——再不就是没等他到手，先从‘酒鬼’身上被人晃走了。…”
好几条汉子咕哝着、哼着抗议。可琼咬定不放，话头也越扯越开。琼问矿场的新闻：干了什么，谁挖着了东西。有人说约翰·阿米蒂奇上来过，从克罗斯家、马栏同比利·布莱恩特手里买了几块好石头。
“阿米蒂奇？”琼说。“他向来是个好主顾——给的价公道。我那批石头是他买的，最后那批……那块大结核给了我一百镑。可小苏菲·鲁米诺夫竟随他跑了，这消息真叫我背过气去。”
话音没落，迈克尔已立到他面前。
“你说什么？”他逼问。
“对不住，迈克尔，”琼飞快、心虚地扫了一圈四周的脸，答道。“可我以为你们当然都知道了。在悉尼，莫德同我常见他们俩在一起；她说开船那天，她在‘泽兰尼达号’（*Zealanida*）上看见苏菲了。莫德下船送朋友，看见苏菲同阿米蒂奇先生都在船上。她说——”
迈克尔沉沉一转身，大步出了酒吧。
琼望着他的背影。从汉子们的脸上，他读出了自己这条消息在人群里炸了多大的一个雷。
“早知道迈克尔不知道，给我多少我也不说这一句，”他说。“可老天，我以为他知道……我以为你们全知道。”
之后那几天，傍晚和夜里在平原上独自游荡时，迈克尔想同自己把发生的事理出个头绪。起初，失落的阴翳罩得太沉，他只明白一件事：苏菲走了。如今，隔着她的走，他又看见了自己控驭局面的努力的溃败。他败了；苏菲走了；她离开了里奇。
“上帝，”他呻吟；“存着世上最好的心，我们把事情搅成多糟的一团！”
迈克尔想：母亲死时，她若随保罗走了，会不会倒更糟。至少，苏菲如今大了些，更能照看自己了。
话虽如此，她就这样说走就走，他还是怪自己；里奇没能留住她，同他自己的失败一起，把他击倒在地。

He had hoped Sophie would care for the things her mother had cared for. He had tried to explain them to her. But Sophie, he thought now, had more the restless temperament of her father. He had not understood her young spirit, its craving for music, laughter, admiration, and the life that could give them to her. He had thought the Ridge would be enough for her, as it had been for her mother.
Michael never thought of Mrs. Rouminof as dead. He thought of her as though she were living some distance from him, that was all. In the evening he looked up at the stars, and there was one in which she seemed to be. Always he felt as if she were looking at him when its mild radiance fell over him. And now he looked to that star as if trying to explain and beg forgiveness.
His heart was sore because Sophie had left him without a word of affection or any explanation. His fear and anxiety for her gave him no peace. He sweated in agony with them for a long time, crying to her mother, praying her to believe he had not failed in his trust through lack of desire to serve her, but through a fault of understanding. If she had been near enough to talk to, he knew he could have explained that the girl was right: neither of them had any right to interfere with the course of her life. She had to go her own way; to learn joy and sorrow for herself.
Too late Michael realised that he had done all the harm in the world by seeking to make Sophie go his own and her mother’s way. He had opposed the tide of her youth and enthusiasm, instead of sympathising with it; and by so doing he had made it possible for someone else to sympathise and help her to go her own way. Opposition had forced her life into channels which he was afraid would heap sorrows upon her, whereas identification with her feeling and aspirations might have saved her the hurt and turmoil he had sought to save her.
Thought of what he had done to prevent Paul taking Sophie away haunted Michael. But, after all, he assured himself, he had not stolen from Paul. Charley had stolen from Paul, and he, Michael, was only holding Paul’s opals until he could give them to Paul when his having them would not do Sophie any harm.﻿ ﻿… His having them now could not injure Sophie.﻿ ﻿… Michael decided to give Paul the opals and explain how he came to have them, when the shock of what Jun had said left him.

===Chinese===

他原盼着苏菲会爱她母亲所爱的东西。他也试着讲给她听过。可如今想来，苏菲身上，父亲那副躁动的性子更多些。他没有懂她那颗少年心——它对音乐、欢笑、倾慕的饥渴，对能给她这些的那种生活的饥渴。他原以为，里奇于她也会够，像于她母亲那样。
迈克尔从不把鲁米诺夫太太当作死者。他想她，只当她住在离他不远不近的一个地方，如此而已。晚上他仰望星星，其中一颗，她仿佛就在上面。它温润的光落下来时，他总觉得她在望着他。此刻他仰望那颗星，像要解释什么，像要讨一份宽恕。
他心口发疼：苏菲走时，没留一句亲爱的话，没有半句解释。对她的怕与忧，叫他不得安生。他为这些淌汗熬煎了很久，朝她母亲哭喊，求她相信：他负了所托，不是不想践诺，而是错在识见。她若近得能说话，他知道他讲得清：这姑娘是对的；他们两个都无权拦她自己一生的路。她得走自己的路；自己尝欢喜，自己尝哀伤。
太迟了，迈克尔才明白：他一心要苏菲走他同她母亲的路，把天下的害都做尽了。他抵了她青春与热望的潮头，而不是顺着它；这样一来，反倒给了另一个人顺着它、帮她走自己路的机会。对抗，把她的生活逼进了那些他生怕会给她堆上哀愁的河槽；而当初若认同她的感情与向往，本可以省下他原想替她省下的那些伤、那些乱。
自己为拦保罗带走苏菲做过的一切，缠着迈克尔不放。可他到底给自己断了案：他没有偷保罗的东西。偷保罗的是查利；他迈克尔不过是替保罗把欧珀存着，等拿到手不再伤苏菲的那一天再还他。……如今他拿着它们，已伤不到苏菲。……迈克尔打定主意：等琼那句话的震退了，就把欧珀还给保罗，讲明它们的来历。

===Original===

He tried not to think of that, although a consciousness of it was always with him.﻿ ﻿… But Paul was delirious with sunstroke, he remembered; it would be foolish to give him the stones just then.﻿ ﻿… As soon as that touch of the sun had passed, Michael reflected, he would give Paul the opals and explain how he came to have them.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

这件事他竭力不去想，虽说那知觉始终随着他。……可保罗正中着暑、说着胡话呢，他记起来；那当口把石头给他，是糊涂。……等这阵暑气一过，迈克尔想，他就把欧珀还给保罗，讲明它们的来历。…

### XVII / 十七
===Original===

When Michael got back to his hut he found Martha there.
“Oh, Michael,” she said, “a dreadful thing has happened.”
Michael stared at her, unable to understand what she said. It seemed to him all the terrible things that could happen had happened that evening.
“While you were away Arthur Henty came here to see Sophie,” Martha said. “She hasn’t been feeling well﻿ ﻿… and I came up to have a look at her. She’s been doing too much lately. Things haven’t been too right between her and Potch, either, and that’s her way of taking it out of herself. Arthur was here when I got here, Michael, and﻿—you never heard anything like the way he went on.﻿ ﻿…”
Michael had fallen wearily into his chair while she was talking.
Martha continued, knowing that the sooner she got rid of her story the better it would be for both of them.
“It’s an old story, of course, this about Arthur Henty and Sophie.﻿ ﻿… When he was ill after the ball he talked a good bit about her.﻿ ﻿… He always has﻿ ﻿… to me. I was with his mother when he was born﻿ ﻿… and he’s always called me Mother M’Cready like the rest of you. He told me long ago he’d always been fond of Sophie.﻿ ﻿… He didn’t know at first, he said. He was a fool; he didn’t like being teased about her.﻿ ﻿… Then she went away.﻿ ﻿… He doesn’t seem to know why he got married except that his people wanted him to.
“After the ball he’d made up his mind they were going away together, Sophie and he. But while he was ill﻿ ﻿… before he was able to get around again, Sophie married Potch. Then he went mad, stark, starin’ mad, and started drinking. He’s been drinking hard ever since.﻿ ﻿… And tonight when he came, he just went over to Sophie.﻿ ﻿… She was lying on the couch under the window, Michael.﻿ ﻿… He said, I’ve got a horse for you outside. Sophie didn’t seem to realise what he meant at first. Then she did. I don’t know how he guessed she wouldn’t go﻿ ﻿… but the next minute he was on his knees beside her﻿ ﻿… and you never heard anything like it, Michael﻿—the way he went on, sobbing and crying out﻿—I never want to hear anything like it again.﻿ ﻿… I couldn’t ’ve stood it meself.﻿ ﻿… I’d ’ve done anything in the world if a man’d gone on to me like that. And Sophie﻿ ﻿… she put her arms round him, and mothered him like.﻿ ﻿… Then she began to cry too.﻿ ﻿… And there they were, both crying and sayin’ how much they loved each other﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

迈克尔回到自己小屋，发现玛莎在那儿。
“哦，迈克尔，”她说，“出大祸了。”
迈克尔瞪着她，一时听不懂。在他看来，那晚天底下的祸事仿佛已经出尽了。
“你不在的时候，阿瑟·亨蒂来这儿找苏菲，”玛莎说。“她这两天身上不爽……我上来看顾她。她近来太操劳了。她同波奇之间也不大对，她就是这么拿折腾自己出气。我到的时候阿瑟正在这儿，迈克尔，还有——他闹的那一场，你没听过那种阵仗。…”
她说话的当口，迈克尔已疲惫地跌进椅子里。
玛莎接着讲：她明白，这故事早一分钟倒出去，对两人都好。
“阿瑟·亨蒂同苏菲这段，当然是老话了。……舞会后他病着那阵，说了她好些。……他一向……对我说。他出生时我在他母亲身边……他也一直同你们大家一样，唤我麦克里迪大妈。他老早就对我说过，他一直喜欢苏菲。……他说，起初他自己也不知道。他是个傻子；人家拿她打趣他，他还不乐意。……后来她走了。……他自己也说不清干吗结婚，只说家里人要他结。
“舞会后他拿定了主意：他要带苏菲一起走。可他病着……还没等他下得了地，苏菲嫁了波奇。他就疯了，彻头彻尾、瞪着眼疯，喝上了酒。从此就没断过。……今晚他一来，径直就到苏菲跟前。……她正躺在窗下那张卧榻上，迈克尔。……他说：我在外头给你备了匹马。苏菲起先像没明白他的意思。随后明白了。不知他怎么猜到她不肯走……下一分钟，他已跪在她身边……那一场你真没听过，迈克尔——他又抽噎又喊——我这辈子再不想听第二回。……换了我，我也撑不住。……一个男人要这样扑在我身上，世上什么事我都依了他。而苏菲……她伸出胳膊搂住他，像当娘的搂孩子。……随后她也哭了。……两个人就那么哭着，说着彼此多爱……

===Original===

how much they’d always loved each other.﻿ ﻿…
“It fair broke me up, Michael.﻿ ﻿… I didn’t know what to do. They didn’t seem to notice me.﻿ ﻿… Then he said again they’d go away together, and begin life all over again. Sophie tried to tell him it was too late to think of that.﻿ ﻿… They both had responsibilities they’d ought to stand by.﻿ ﻿… Hers was the Ridge and the Ridge life, she said.﻿ ﻿… He didn’t understand.﻿ ﻿… He only understood he wanted her to go away with him, and she wouldn’t go.﻿ ﻿…”
Michael was so spent in body and mind that what Martha was saying did not at first make any impression on his mind. She seemed to be telling him a long and dolorous tale of something which had happened a long time ago, to people he had once known. In a waking nightmare, realisation that it was Sophie she was talking of dawned on him.
“He tried to make her,” Martha was saying when he began to listen intently. “He said he’d been weak and a fool all his days. But he wasn’t any more. He was strong now. He knew what he wanted, and he meant to have it.﻿ ﻿… Sophie was his, he said. Nothing in the world would ever make her anything but his. She knew it, and he knew it.﻿ ﻿… And Sophie hid her face in her hands. He took her hands away from her face and dragged her to her feet. He asked her if he was her mate.
“She said ‘Yes.’
“ ‘Then you’ve got to come with me,’ he said.
“But she wouldn’t go, Michael. She tried to explain it was the Ridge﻿—what the Ridge stood for﻿—she must stay to work for. She’d sworn to, she said. He cursed the Ridge and all of us, Michael. He said that he wouldn’t let her go on living with Potch﻿—be his wife. That he’d kill her, and himself, and Potch, rather than let her.﻿ ﻿… I never heard a man go on like he did, Michael. I never want to again. Half the time he was raging mad, then crying like a child. But in the end he said, quite quietly:
“ ‘Will you come with me, Sophie?’
“And she said, quiet like that, too, ‘No.’
“He went out of the hut.﻿ ﻿… I heard him ride away. Sophie cried after him. She put out her arms﻿ ﻿… but she couldn’t speak. And if you had seen her face, Michael﻿—She just stood there against the wall, listening to the hoof-beats.﻿ ﻿… When we couldn’t hear them any more, she stood there listening just the same. I went to her and tried to﻿—to waken her﻿—she seemed to have gone off into a sort of trance, Michael.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

说着一向爱得多么深。…
“这一场真把我的心搅碎了，迈克尔。……我不知怎么办。他们俩像没瞧见我。……随后他又说，要带她走，把日子重新起头。苏菲想说：想这个已经太迟了。……两人都有各自的责任，都得守住。……她的，是里奇、是里奇的日子，她说。……他不懂。……他只懂他要她走，她不肯。…”
迈克尔的身心都耗空了，玛莎的话起初进不了他的脑子。她像在对他讲一个又长又惨的老故事，讲的是许多年前、他从前认得的一些人。一场醒着的梦魇里，他渐渐醒豁：她讲的是苏菲。
“他逼她来着，”他凝神细听时，玛莎正讲到这儿。“他说他这辈子又弱又傻。可如今不了。他如今强壮了。他知道自己要什么，也拿定了要到手。……苏菲是他的，他说。世上没有什么能叫她不成他的。她知道，他也知道。……苏菲把脸埋进手里。他掰开她的手，把她拽起来。问她，他算不算她的伙计。
“她说‘算’。
“‘那你就得跟我走，’他说。
“可她不肯走，迈克尔。她想解释：是里奇——里奇所代表的那些——她必须留下为之出力。她起过誓，她说。他咒里奇、咒我们所有人，迈克尔。他说他不许她再跟波奇过——做他的老婆。宁肯杀了她、杀了他自己、杀了波奇，也不许。……我没听过一个男人这样闹，迈克尔。再不想听第二回。他一半时间狂怒，转眼又哭得像个孩子。可末了，他安安静静地说：
“‘你肯跟我走吗，苏菲？’
“她也那样安安静静地说：‘不肯。’
“他出了屋。……我听见他骑马走了。苏菲朝他哭喊。她伸出胳膊……却说不出话。你要是见了她那张脸，迈克尔——她只倚着墙立在那里，听马蹄声。……听不见了，她还立在那儿听。我走过去，想——想唤醒她——她像掉进了一种迷睡里，迈克尔。…

===Original===

After a while she did wake; but she looked at me as if she didn’t know me. She walked about for a bit, she walked round the table, and then she went out as though she were goin’ for a walk. I told her not to go far﻿ ﻿… not to be long﻿ ﻿… but I don’t think she heard me.﻿ ﻿… I watched her walking out towards the old rush.﻿ ﻿… And she isn’t back yet.﻿ ﻿…”
“It’s too much,” Michael muttered.
He sat with his head buried in his hands.
“What’s to be done about it?” he asked at last.
Martha shook her head.
“I don’t know. Sophie’ll go through with her part, I suppose﻿ ﻿… as her mother did.”
Michael’s face quivered.
“He’s such an outsider,” he groaned. “Sophie’d never give up the things we stand for here, now she understands them.”
“That’s just it,” Martha said. “She doesn’t want to﻿—but there’s something stronger than herself draggin’ at her﻿ ﻿… it’s something that’s been in all the women she’s come of﻿—the feeling a woman’s got for the man who’s her mate. Sophie married Potch, it’s my belief, to get away from this man. She wanted to chain herself to us and her life here. She wants to stay with us.﻿ ﻿… She was kept up at first by ideas of duty and sacrifice, and serving something more than her own happiness. But love’s like murder, Michael﻿—it must out, and it’s a good thing it must.﻿ ﻿…”
“And what about Potch?” Michael asked.
“Potch?” Martha smiled. “The dear lad﻿ ﻿… he’ll stand up to things. There are people like that﻿—and there’re people like Arthur Henty who can’t stand up to things. It’s not their fault they’re made that way﻿ ﻿… and they go under when they have too much to bear.”
“Curse him,” Michael groaned. “I wish he’d kept out of our lives.”
“So do I,” Martha said; “but he hasn’t.”
Potch came in. He looked from Martha to Michael.
“Where’s Sophie?” he asked.
“She﻿ ﻿… went out for a walk, a while ago,” Martha said.
At first Martha believed Potch knew what had happened. In his eyes there was an awe and horror which communicated itself to Martha and Michael, and held them dumb.
“Henty has shot himself down in the tank paddock,” he said at length.
Martha uttered a low wail. Michael looked at Potch, waiting to hear further.
“Some of the boys going home to the Three Mile heard the shot, and went over,” Potch said. “I wanted to tell Sophie myself.﻿ ﻿… They were looking for you in the town, Martha.”
“Oh!” Martha got up and went to the door.

===Chinese===

过了一阵她真醒了；可她望我那样子，像不认得我。她走了一阵，绕着桌子走，随后出了门，像寻常散步那样。我嘱她别走远……早点回……可她像没听见。……我望着她朝老抢地场那边去了。……到现在还没回。…”
“太多了。”迈克尔喃喃。
他坐着，头埋在手里。
“这可怎么办？”他终于问。
玛莎摇头。
“不知道。苏菲会把她那一份担到底，我料想……像她母亲那样。”
迈克尔的脸抽搐了一下。
“他这么一个外路货，”他呻吟。“苏菲如今懂了咱们在这儿守的东西，绝不会丢下它。”
“正是这话，”玛莎说。“她不想丢——可有一样比她自己更硬的东西拽着她……那是她身上传下来的所有女人都有的——一个女人对她男人、她伙计的那份情。苏菲嫁波奇，我相信，就是为了躲这个人。她要拿锁链把自己锁进咱们、锁进这边的日子。她想留下。……起初撑着她的，是责任、牺牲、效劳比一己幸福更大那些念头。可爱情这东西，迈克尔，就同谋杀一样——藏不住的，藏住才不是好事。…”
“那波奇呢？”迈克尔问。
“波奇？”玛莎笑了。“这可爱的孩子……他顶得住。世上就有这样的人——也有阿瑟·亨蒂那种顶不住的。生就那样，怪不得他们……担子一重，他们就沉下去。”
“该死的，”迈克尔呻吟。“他干吗不滚出咱们的日子。”
“我也这么想，”玛莎说；“可他没有。”
波奇进来了。他的眼睛从玛莎挪到迈克尔。
“苏菲呢？”他问。
“她……出去散步了，有一阵了。”玛莎说。
起初玛莎还当波奇已经知道出了事。他眼里那种敬畏与骇怖，传给了玛莎同迈克尔，把两人都钉住了。
“亨蒂在蓄水塘围场开枪打了自己。”他终于说。
玛莎低低哀了一声。迈克尔望着波奇，等下文。
“几个回三里的小伙子听见了枪响，赶了过去，”波奇说。“我想自己告诉苏菲。……镇上正找你呢，玛莎。”
“哦！”玛莎起身走到门口。

===Original===

“He’s at Newton’s,” Potch said. “Which way did Sophie go?”
“She went towards the Old Town, Potch,” Martha said.
The chestnut Arthur Henty had brought for Sophie, still standing with reins over a post of the goat-pen, whinnied when he saw them at the door of the hut. Potch looked at him as if he were wondering why the horse was there﻿—a vague perplexity defined itself through the troubled abstraction of his gaze. His eyes went to Martha as if asking her how the horse came to be there; but she did not offer any explanation. She went off down the track to Newton’s, and he struck out towards the Old Town.
Potch wandered over the plains looking for Sophie. She was not in any of her usual haunts. He wandered, looking for her, calling her, wondering what this news would mean to her. Vaguely, instinctively he knew. From the time of their marriage nothing had been said between them of Arthur Henty.
“Sophie! Sophie!” he called.
The stars were swarming points of silver fire in the blue-black sky. He wandered, calling still. Desolation overwhelmed him because he could not find Sophie; because she was in none of the places they had spent so much time in together. It was significant that she should not be in any of them, he felt. He could not bear to think she was eluding him, and yet that was what she had done all her life. She had been with him, smiling, elfish and tender one moment, and gone the next. She had always been elusive. For a long time a presentiment of desolation and disaster had overshadowed him. Again and again he had been able to draw breath of relief and assure himself that the indefinable dread which was always with him was a chimera of his too absorbing, too anxious love. But the fear, instinctive, prophetic, begotten by consciousness of the slight grasp he had of her, had remained.
That morning even, before he had gone off to work, she had taken his face in her hands. He had seen tenderness and an infinite gentleness in her eyes.
“Dear Potch,” she had said, and kissed him.
She had withdrawn from him before the faint chill which her words and the light pressure of her lips diffused, had left him. And now he was wandering over the plains looking for her, calling her.﻿ ﻿… He had done so before.﻿ ﻿… Sophie liked to wander off like this by herself.

===Chinese===

“他在牛顿家，”波奇说。“苏菲往哪边去了？”
“往老镇那边去了，波奇。”玛莎说。
阿瑟·亨蒂给苏菲带来的那匹栗毛马，还立在羊圈栏桩旁，缰绳搭在桩上；一见他们出现在小屋门口，就打了个响鼻。波奇望着它，像奇怪它怎么会在这儿——一层模糊的困惑，从他惶惶出神的目光里透了出来。他的眼睛转向玛莎，像问她这马打哪儿来；可她没有解释。她顺小径下牛顿家去了；他迈步直奔老镇。
波奇在平原上四处找苏菲。她惯常待的地方，一处也没有。他走着，找着，唤着，心里掂着这个消息对她会意味着什么。他模模糊糊、凭本能地知道。成亲以来，两人中间谁也没提过阿瑟·亨蒂。
“苏菲！苏菲！”他唤。
星星在蓝黑的天上聚成点点银火。他走着，还在唤。找不见苏菲的荒凉压倒了他；他们共度了那么多时光的地方，一处也没有她。她不在任何一处——这本身就说明着什么，他觉出来了。他不敢想她在躲他；可她一辈子都在躲他。她同他在一起，这一刻笑着，精灵似的、温存可人，下一刻就没了。她一直是抓不住的。很久以来，一种荒凉与灾祸的预感就罩着他。他一次次缓过一口气来，安慰自己说：那份总跟着他的、说不清的怕，不过是他的爱太满、太焦结出的幻影。可那份怕——本能的、预言的、由“他抓她抓得那样浅”这一知觉生出的怕——一直都在。
就在今天早晨，他上工之前，她还双手捧住他的脸。他看见她眼里一派温存、无限的轻柔。
“亲爱的波奇，”她说，吻了他。
她的话、她唇上那一点轻轻的凉意还没散，她的人已从他身边退了出去。此刻他在平原上走，找她，唤她。……从前也有过这样的日子。……苏菲就爱这样一个人跑开。

===Original===

Sometimes he had found her in a place where they often sat together; sometimes she had been in the hut before him; sometimes she had come in a long time after him, wearily, a strange, remote expression on her face, as if long gazing at the stars or into the darkness which overhung the plains had deprived her of some earthliness.
He did not know how long he walked over the plains and along the Ridge, looking for her, his soul in that cry:
“Sophie! Sophie!”
He wandered for hours before he went back to the hut, and saw Michael coming out to meet him.
“She knows, Potch,” Michael said.
Potch waited for him to continue.
“Says nobody told her.﻿ ﻿… She heard the shot﻿ ﻿… and knew,” Michael said.
Potch exclaimed brokenly. He asked how Sophie was. Michael said she had come in and had lain down on the sofa as though she were very tired. She had been lying there ever since, so still that Michael was alarmed. He had called Paul and sent him to find Martha. Sophie had not cried at all, Michael said.
She was lying on the sofa under the window, her hair thrown back from her face when Potch went into the hut. He closed his eyes against the sight of her face; he could not see Sophie in the grip of such pain. He knelt beside her.
“Sophie! Sophie!” he murmured, the inarticulate prayer of his love and anguish in those words.

===Chinese===

有时他会在两人常坐的地方找到她；有时她比他先回屋；有时他回来很久她才回，倦倦的，脸上一副陌生的、遥远的神情，仿佛久望着星星、或久望着罩住平原的黑暗，被抽走了几分地气。
他在平原上、顺着里奇走了多久找她，自己也不知道；魂灵全在那一声喊里：
“苏菲！苏菲！”
他走了几个钟头才回小屋，见迈克尔正出来迎他。
“她知道了，波奇。”迈克尔说。
波奇等他说下去。
“说没人告诉她。……她听见了枪声……就知道了。”迈克尔说。
波奇破碎地叫了一声，问苏菲怎么样。迈克尔说，她进屋就躺上了沙发，像累极了。从那时起一直躺着，静得叫迈克尔心慌。他已叫来保罗，打发他去找玛莎。苏菲一滴泪也没掉，迈克尔说。
波奇进屋时，她躺在窗下沙发上，头发从脸上向后抛着。他闭上眼，不敢看那张脸；他受不了苏菲攥在这样一种痛里。他跪到她身旁。
“苏菲！苏菲！”他喃喃，他的爱与苦楚那说不成句的祷告，全在这两声里。

### XVIII / 十八
===Original===

The men met to talk about Michael next evening. The meeting was informal, but every man on the fields had come to Fallen Star for it. The hall was filled to the doors as it had been the night before, but the crowd had none of the elastic excitement and fighting spirit, the antagonisms and enthusiasms, which had gone off from it in wavelike vibrations the night before. News of Arthur Henty’s death had left everybody aghast, and awakened realisation of the abysses which even a life that seemed to move easily could contain. The shock of it was on everybody; the solemnity it had created in the air.
George Woods, elected spokesman for the men, and Roy O’Mara deputed to take notes of the meeting because he was reckoned to be a good penman, sat at a table on the platform. Michael took a chair just below the platform, facing the men. He was there to answer questions. No one had asked him to be present, but it was the custom when men of the Ridge were holding an inquiry of the sort for the man or men concerned to have seats in front of the platform, and Michael had gone to sit there as soon as the men were in their places.
“This isn’t like any other inquiry we’ve had on the Ridge,” George Woods said. “You chaps know how I feel about it﻿—I told you last night. But Michael was for it, and I take it he’s come here to answer any questions﻿ ﻿… and to clear this thing up once and for all.﻿ ﻿… He’s put his case to you. He says he’ll stand by what you say﻿—the judgment of his mates.”
Anxious to spare Michael another recital of what had happened, he went on:
“There’s no need for Michael to repeat what he said last night. If there’s any man here wasn’t in the hall, these are the facts.”
He repeated the story Michael had told, steadily, clearly, and impartially.
“If there’s any man wants to ask a question on those facts, he can do it now.”
George sat down, and M’Ginnis was on his feet the same instant; his bat-like ears twitching, his shoulders hunched, his whole tall, thin frame strung to the pitch of nervous animosity.
“I want to know,” he said, “what reason there is for believing a word of it. Michael Brady’s as good as admitted he’s been fooling you for goodness knows how long, and I don’t see﻿—”
“Y’ soon will, y’r bleedin’, blasted, flyblown fool,” Bully Bryant roared, rising and pushing back his sleeves.
“Sit down, Bull,” George Woods called.

===Chinese===

第二天晚上，人们聚在一起谈迈克尔的事。聚会是非正式的，可矿场上人人都赶到流星岭来了。礼堂挤到门口，同前一夜一样；可人群里已没有昨夜那种弹性的兴奋与斗志、那种对抗与热忱——昨夜它们曾如波纹般从人群里荡出去。亚瑟·亨蒂的死讯把每个人都震得目瞪口呆，唤醒了一种领悟：哪怕一段看似走得平顺的人生，也暗藏着深渊。这震动人人身上都有；它投在空气里的那份肃穆也在。
乔治·伍兹被推为众人发言人，罗伊·奥马拉因公认一笔好字被派做会议记录，两人坐在台上的一张桌边。迈克尔在台侧下方占了把椅子，面向众人。他是来答问的。没人请他到场，但里奇人开这种查问时，惯例是当事的一人或数人坐在前台；男人们刚各就各位，迈克尔便去坐下了。
"这同咱们里奇开过的任何查问都不一样，"乔治·伍兹说。"弟兄们知道我对这事的心情——昨晚说过了。可迈克尔本人愿意开这个会，我料他是来答问的……并且来把这件事一劳永逸地弄清楚。……他已把他的案情摆给你们了。他说，你们说什么他都认——他伙伴们的裁断。"
他急于免得迈克尔再复述一遍出事经过，便接着说：
"用不着迈克尔再重复昨晚的话了。这儿要有人昨晚不在场，事实如下。"
他把迈克尔讲过的经过重述了一遍，平稳、清楚、不偏不倚。
"这些事实要是有人要问，现在就问。"
乔治坐下，麦金尼斯同一瞬间跳了起来；蝙蝠似的耳朵抽动，肩膀拱起，瘦高的整个身架绷到敌意的顶点。
"我想知道，"他说，"凭什么相信这里头一个字。迈克尔·布雷迪差不多等于承认，他天知道蒙了你们多久，我看不出——"
"你马上就会看出来的，你这个遭瘟的、生蛆的蠢货，"布利·布莱恩特吼道，站起身挽袖子。
"坐下，布利。"乔治·伍兹喊。

===Original===

“The question is,” he added, “what reason is there for believing what Michael says?”
“His word’s enough,” somebody called.
“Some of us think so,” George said. “But there’s some don’t. Is there anyone else can say, Michael?”
Michael shook his head. He thought of Snowshoes, but the old man had refused to be present at the inquiry or to have anything to do with it. He had pretended to be deaf when he was asked anything about Paul’s opals. And Michael, who could only surmise that Snowshoes’ reasons for having taken the stones in a measure resembled his own when he took them from Paul, would not have him put to the torture of questioning.
George had said: “It might make a lot of difference to Michael if you’d come along, Mr. Riley.”
But Snowshoes had marched off from him as if he had not heard anyone speak, his blue eyes fixed on that invisible goal he was always gazing at and going towards.
George had not seen him come into the hall; but when he was needed, his tall figure, white clad and straight as a dead tree, rose at the back of the hall.
“It’s true,” he said. “I wanted to be sure of Michael; I shadowed him. I saw him with the stones when he says. I did not see him with them any other time.”
He sat down again; his eyes, which had flashed, resumed their steady, distant stare; his features relapsed into their mask of impassivity.
M’Ginnis sprang to his feet again.
“That’s all very well,” he cried, sticking to his question. “But it’s not my idea of evidence. It wouldn’t stand in any law court in the country. Snowshoes﻿—”
“Shut up!”
“Sit down!”
Half a dozen voices growled.
Because of the respect and affection they had for him, and because of a certain aloof dignity he had with them, no man on the Ridge ever addressed Snowshoes as anything but Mr. Riley. They resented M’Ginnis calling him “Snowshoes” to his face, and guessed that he had been going to say something which would reflect on Snowshoes’ reliability as a witness. They admitted his eccentricity; but they would not admit that his mental peculiarities amounted to more than that. Above all, they were not going to have his feelings hurt by this outsider from the Punti rush.
Broad-shouldered, square and solid, Bill Grant towered above the men about him.

===Chinese===

"问题在于，"他补一句，"凭什么相信迈克尔的话？"
"他一句话就够了。"有人喊。
"咱们中间多数人这么想，"乔治说。"可有少数人不。还有别人能作证吗，迈克尔？"
迈克尔摇头。他想到"雪鞋"（Snowshoes），可老人拒绝了，不肯到会，也不肯沾边。凡问到保罗的蛋白石，他都装聋。而迈克尔——只能揣测"雪鞋"当初收下那些石头的用意，多少同自己把它们从保罗那里拿走时的相仿——不肯让老人受盘问的刑。
乔治曾说："您要是肯来一趟，对迈克尔兴许大不一样，赖利先生。"
可"雪鞋"从他面前径直走开了，仿佛没听见谁说话，蓝眼睛钉在他永远凝望、永远走向的那个看不见的目标上。
乔治没看见他进礼堂；可等到需要他时，他那穿白衣、直得像枯树的高个子身影，从礼堂后头立了起来。
"是真的，"他说。"我要弄准迈克尔的人品；我盯了他。他说的那个时辰，我看见他带着那些石头。别的时候我没见过。"
他重新坐下；闪过的那点亮回到他一贯的、悠远的凝视里；五官重新落回那副无动于衷的面具。
麦金尼斯又蹦了起来。
"这很好，"他叫道，咬定他的问题不放。"可这不算我认的证据。这在这个国家的任何法庭上都立不住。雪鞋——"
"闭嘴！"
"坐下！"
五六条嗓子齐声低吼。
出于对老人的敬与爱，也出于他在他们中间自有一份孤高的尊严，里奇人提起"雪鞋"，从来只称赖利先生。麦金尼斯当面叫他"雪鞋"，众人立刻生恨，而且猜到他正要说些有损"雪鞋"证人信誉的话。他们承认他古怪；可不能承认他的精神特质越过了"古怪"一步。尤其不能容的，是这个蓬蒂来的外来客伤他的感情。
宽肩方正面敦实的比尔·格兰特，从周围的人中间巍然立起。

===Original===

“This doesn’t pretend to be a court of law, Mister M’Ginnis,” he remarked, with an irony and emphasis which never failed of their mark when he used them, although he rarely did, and only once or twice had been heard to speak, at any gathering. “It’s an inquiry by men of the Ridge into the doings of one of their mates. What they want to know is the rights of this business﻿ ﻿… and what you consider evidence doesn’t matter. It’s what the men in this hall consider evidence matters. And, what’s more, I don’t see why you’re butting into our affairs so much: you’re not one of us﻿—you’re a newcomer. You’ve only been a year or so in the place﻿ ﻿… and this concerns only men of the Ridge, who stand by the Ridge ways of doing things.﻿ ﻿… Michael’s here to be judged by his mates﻿ ﻿… not by you and your sort.﻿ ﻿… If you’d the brain of a louse, you’d understand﻿—this isn’t a question of law, but of principle﻿—honour, if you like to call it that.”
“Does the meeting consider the question answered?” George Woods inquired when Bill Grant sat down.
“Yes!”
A chorus of voices intoned the answer.
“If you believe Michael’s story, there’s nothing more to be said,” George continued. “Does any man want to ask Michael a question?”
No one replied for a moment. Then M’Ginnis exclaimed incoherently.
“Shut up!”
“Sit down!”
Men cried out all over the hall.
“That’s all, I think, Michael,” George said, looking down to where Michael sat before the platform; and Michael, pulling his hat further over his eyes, went out of the hall.
It was the custom for men of the Ridge to talk over the subject of their inquiry together after the man or men with whom the meeting was concerned had left the hall, before giving their verdict.
When Michael had gone, George Woods said:
“The boys would like to hear what you’ve got to say, I think, Archie.”
He looked at Archie Cross. “You and Michael haven’t been seein’ eye to eye lately, and if there’s any other side in this business, it’s the side that lost confidence in Michael when we were fed-up with all that whispering. You know Michael, and you’re a good Ridge man, though you were ready to take on Armitage’s scheme. The boys’d like to hear what you’ve got to say, I’m sure.”
Archie Cross stood up; he rolled his hat in his hands.

===Chinese===

"这并不是要冒充什么法庭，麦金尼斯先生，"他开口，那种讥讽与加重，他一用必中，可他难得用，任何集会上人们听到他开口统共不过一两回。"这是里奇人对一个伙伴的作为的查问。他们想知道的是这件事的是非……你认什么证据，无关紧要。要紧的是这座礼堂里的人认什么证据。再者，我不明白你为什么这么起劲地往咱们的事里拱：你不是我们的人——你是个新来的。你到这地方才一年上下……而这只关里奇人的事，只关站得住里奇规矩的人。……迈克尔是来受他伙伴们的裁断的……不是受你和你们那一路人的。……你要是有只虱子的脑子，就会懂——这不是法律问题，是原则问题——体面问题，你愿意这么叫也行。"
"到会的人认为问题回答了吗？"比尔·格兰特坐下后，乔治·伍兹问。
"回答了！"
一片齐声应和。
"既然你们信迈克尔的话，就没什么可说的了，"乔治接着说。"还有人要问迈克尔什么吗？"
一时无人应。随后麦金尼斯语无伦次地又嚷起来。
"闭嘴！"
"坐下！"
满堂的人齐喝。
"我看就这样了，迈克尔。"乔治望着台前的迈克尔说；迈克尔把帽子往下拉了拉，出了礼堂。
按里奇的习惯，当事人离场后、下裁断前，到会的人要把查问的题目合议一遍。
迈克尔走后，乔治·伍兹说：
"弟兄们想听听你怎么说，我想，阿奇。"他看向阿奇·克罗斯。"你同迈克尔近来不大对付；这桩事里要真还有另一头，那便是咱们叫那些闲话灌满耳朵、对迈克尔失了信心的那一头。你了解迈克尔，你也是个好里奇人，虽说你一度打算接阿米蒂奇的方案。弟兄们想听听你怎么说，我敢保。"
阿奇·克罗斯站起来；他把帽子在手里卷来卷去。

===Original===

His face, hacked out of a piece of dull flesh, sun-reddened, moved convulsively; his hair was roughed-up from it; his small, sombre eyes went with straight lightnings to the men in the hall about him.
“It’s true﻿—what George says,” he said after a pause, as if it were difficult for him to express his thought. “I haven’t been seein’ eye to eye with Michael lately﻿ ﻿… and I listened to all the dirty gossip that mob”﻿—he glanced towards M’Ginnis and the men with him﻿—“put round about him. It was part that﻿ ﻿… and part listening to their talk about money invested here making all the difference to Fallen Star﻿ ﻿… and the children growing up﻿ ﻿… and gettin’ scared and worried about seein’ them through﻿ ﻿… made me go agin you boys lately, and let that lot get hold of me.﻿ ﻿… But this business about Michael’s shown me where I am. Michael’s stood for one thing all through﻿—the Ridge and the hanging on to the mines for us.﻿ ﻿… He’s been a better Ridge man than I have.﻿ ﻿… And I want to say﻿ ﻿… as far as I’m concerned, Michael’s proved himself.﻿ ﻿… I don’t reck’n hanging on to opals was anything﻿ ﻿… no more does Ted. It’s the sort of thing a chap like Michael’d do absentminded﻿ ﻿… not noticin’ what he was doin’; but when he did notice﻿—and got scared thinkin’ where he was gettin’ to, and what it might look like, he couldn’t get rid of ’em quick, enough. That’s what I think, and that’s what Ted thinks, too. He hasn’t got the gift of the gab, Ted, or he’d say so himself.﻿ ﻿… If there’s goin’ to be opposition to Michael, it’s not comin’ from us.﻿ ﻿… And we’ve made up our minds we stand by the Ridge.”
“Good old Archie!” somebody shouted.
“What have you got to say, Roy?” George Woods faced his secretary who had been scratching diligently throughout the meeting. “You’ve been more with the M’Ginnis lot, too, than with us, lately.”
Roy flushed and sprang to his feet.
“I’m in the same boat with Archie and Ted,” he said. “Except about the family﻿ ﻿… mine isn’t so big yet as it might be. But it’s a fact, I funked, not having had much luck lately.﻿ ﻿… But if ever I go back on the Ridge again﻿ ﻿… may the lot of you go back on me.”
Exclamations of approbation and goodwill reverberated as Roy subsided into his chair again.
“That’s all there is to be said on the subject, I think,” George Woods remarked.
“Michael wanted his mates to know what he had done﻿—and why he had done it.

他那像从一块黯肉里劈出来的脸，晒得通红，抽搐着；头发支棱着；一双阴沉的小眼睛带着笔直的闪电射向满堂的人。
"是实——乔治说的，"他顿了顿才说，仿佛表达思想于他是件难事。"我近来同迈克尔是不大对付……那帮家伙——"他朝麦金尼斯和他那伙人瞥了一眼——"散布的脏闲话，我全听了。一半为这个……一半是听他们念叨，投下资来流星岭就翻身……孩子一年年大……想到拉扯他们成人，心里发慌发愁……叫我近来背离了你们弟兄，叫那伙人攥住了我。……可迈克尔这桩事，让我看清了自己站在哪儿。迈克尔从头到尾只认一样东西——里奇，替咱们死死攥住矿山。……他这个里奇人，当得比我强。……我要说……在我这头，迈克尔已经证明了他自己。……我从不觉得囤着蛋白石算个事……特德也不觉得。这是迈克尔那种人浑然不觉干出来的事……没留意自己在干什么；可一旦留意了——想到自己在往哪儿滑、这会招人怎么看，心里一怕，就巴不得赶紧把它们脱手。我是这么想的，特德也是。特德嘴笨，不然他自己会说。……要还有人跟迈克尔作对，也轮不到我们。……我们拿定了主意：站在里奇这一边。"
"好样的阿奇！"有人喊。
"你有什么要说的，罗伊？"乔治·伍兹面向他的记录——整场会他都在勤勤恳恳地划。"你近来同麦金尼斯那伙人也比同我们走得近。"
罗伊红了脸，一跃而起。
"我同阿奇、特德一条船，"他说。"除了家口那一层……我家的还没他们的大。可实情是，我怊过——近来手气又不顺。……可我往后要再背叛里奇……就叫你们大伙儿一齐背叛我。"
罗伊坐回椅子，满堂喝彩与善意嗡嗡回荡。
"这题目上要说的就这些了，我看。"乔治·伍兹说。
"迈克尔要他的伙伴们知道他干了什么——为什么干。

===Original===

He’s asked for judgment from his mates.﻿ ﻿… If he’d wanted to go back on us he could have done it; he could have done it quite easy. Armitage would have shut up on his suspicions about the stones. Charley could have been bought. Michael need never ’ve faced all this as far as I can see﻿ ﻿… but he decided to face it rather than give up all we’ve been fightin’ for here. He’d rather take all the dirt we care to sling at him than anything they could give him﻿ ﻿… and that’s why M’Ginnis has been up against him like he has. Michael has queered his pitch, and most of us have a notion that M’Ginnis has been here to do Armitage’s work﻿ ﻿… work up discontent and ill-feeling amongst us, and split our ranks; and he came very near doing it. If Michael hadn’t ’ve stood by us, like he’s always done, we’d have the Armitage Syndicate on our backs by now.”
“To tell you the truth, boys,” George went on, after a moment’s hesitation, and then as if the impulse to speak a secret thought were too strong for him, “I’ve always thought Michael was too good. And if those stones did get hold of him for a couple of weeks, like he says, all it proves, as far as I can see, is that Michael isn’t any plaster saint, but a man like the rest of us.”
“That’s right!” Watty called, and several men shouted after him.
Pony-Fence moved out from the crowd he was sitting with.
“I vote this meeting records a motion of confidence in Michael Brady,” he said. “And when we call Michael in again we’d ought to make it clear to him﻿ ﻿… that so far from its being a question of not having as much confidence in him as we had before﻿—we’ve got more. Michael’s stood by his mates if ever a man did.﻿ ﻿… He’s come to us﻿ ﻿… he’s given himself up to us. He’ll stand by what we say or do about him. And what are we goin’ to do? Are we goin’ to turn him down﻿ ﻿… read him a bit of a lecture and tell him to go home and be a good boy and not do it another time﻿ ﻿… or are we going to let him know once and for all what we think of him?”
Exclamations of agreement went up in a rabble of voices.
Bully Bryant rose from one of the back forms with a grin which illuminated the building.
“I’ll second that motion,” he said, pushing back the sleeve on his left arm. “And his own mother won’t know the man who says a word against it﻿—when I’ve done with him.”
Watty was sent to bring Michael back to the meeting.

===Chinese===

他要伙伴们给他裁断。……他若想背弃我们，他背弃得了；轻而易举。阿米蒂奇对石头的疑心本可以咽下去。查利本可以买通。照我看，迈克尔本可以永远不必面对这一切……可他宁可面对它，也不肯丢掉咱们在这儿拼死守着的一切。咱们爱朝他泼多少脏水，他都认，胜过他们能给他的一切……这也正是麦金尼斯一直跟他过不去的缘由。迈克尔搅了他的局；咱们多数人都有个数：麦金尼斯到这儿来，就是替阿米蒂奇办事的……在咱们中间鼓动不满与恶感，分裂咱们的队伍；他差点办成了。要不是迈克尔像一贯那样死死站在咱们这边，阿米蒂奇辛迪加这会儿早骑在咱们脖子上了。"
"跟弟兄们说实话，"乔治顿了顿，又像憋不住一句心腹话似的，接着说，"我一向觉得，迈克尔太好了。要是那些石头真像他说的那样攥了他两个星期，那么照我看，这只证明一件事：迈克尔不是什么石膏圣人，是同咱们一样的人。"
"正是！"瓦蒂喊，随后好几个男人跟着喊。
"马栏"从他坐着的人堆里挪出来。
"我提议，本会记录一项对迈克尔·布雷迪的信任案，"他说。"回头再把迈克尔叫进来，咱们应当向他讲明……事情远不是'对他不如从前那么信'的问题——咱们是更信了。迈克尔像天底下顶硬气的汉子那样，没撇下他的伙伴。……他来找咱们……把自己交给咱们。咱们怎么说他、怎么裁他，他都认。可咱们打算怎么着？是要把他一脚踢开……训他一顿、打发他回家做个乖孩子、下不为例……还是要一次性让他知道，咱们把他看成什么样的人？"
乱哄哄一片赞同炸了开来。
布利·布莱恩特从后排长凳上起身，咧嘴一笑，照亮了整座屋子。
"我附议，"他说，挽起左臂的袖子。"谁敢说半个不字，等我收拾完，他亲娘都认不出他来。"
瓦蒂被派去把迈克尔请回会场。

===Original===

They walked to the end of the hall together; and George Woods told Michael as quietly as he could for his own agitation, and the joy which, welling in him, impeded his speech, that men of the Ridge found nothing to censure in what he had done. His mates believed in him; they stood by him. They were prepared to stand by him as he had stood by the Ridge always. The meeting wished to record a vote of confidence.﻿ ﻿…
Cheers roared to the roof. Michael, shaken by the storm of his emotion and gratitude, stood before the crowd in the hall with bowed head. When the storm was quieter in him, he lifted his head and looked out to the men, his eyes shining with tears.
He could not speak; old mates closed round to shake hands with him before the meeting broke up. Every man grasped and wrung his hand, saying:
“Good luck! Good luck to you, Michael!” Or just grasped his hand and smiled with that assurance of fellowship and goodwill which meant more to Michael than anything else in the world.

===Chinese===

两人一同走到礼堂尽头；乔治·伍兹尽量平静地告诉迈克尔——他自己的激动和涌上心头的欢喜堵着他的话——里奇人在他所做的事里找不出可责之处。他的伙伴们信他；他们撑他。他们准备像他一贯撑里奇那样撑他。到会同仁愿记录一项信任表决。……
欢呼冲上屋顶。迈克尔被情感与感激的风暴摇撼着，垂头立在满堂人前。等心里的风暴平息了些，他抬起头，望向众人，眼睛闪着泪光。
他说不出话；散会前，老伙伴们围拢来同他握手。每个人都攥住他的手，用力摇着，说：
"祝好运！祝你好运，迈克尔！"或者只是握住他的手，笑着，带着那份同志与善意的笃定——对迈克尔，它贵过世上一切。
